# Porting ledger

This file tracks which upstream iOS files have been ported to Kotlin, where each one lives
in this project, and every place the port deliberately differs from upstream.

Upstream is [`perezjuanj/OpenCircuit`](https://github.com/perezjuanj/OpenCircuit) (MIT,
© 2026 Juan Perez). The commit the port currently tracks is recorded in
[`UPSTREAM.md`](UPSTREAM.md); that file is the only source of the pin. The `SHA` column
below records the upstream commit each row was ported from.

## Porting rule

- Port each upstream Swift source file **together with its test file**. The ported test is
  run and shown failing before the Kotlin source exists, then passing after.
- A module is done only when its ported upstream test vectors pass.
- The protocol reference is upstream's [`docs/PROTOCOL.md`](../docs/PROTOCOL.md). Where it
  disagrees with the Swift code, the protocol document wins.
- Byte values are `Int` 0–255 read through one unsigned helper; byte sequences are
  `ByteArray`; time is `java.time.Instant`. Kotlin unsigned types are not used.

Paths: upstream `S/` = `ios/OpenCircuitKit/Sources/OpenCircuitKit/`, `T/` =
`ios/OpenCircuitKit/Tests/OpenCircuitKitTests/`, `A/` = `ios/OpenCircuit/` (the app),
`AT/` = `ios/OpenCircuitTests/` (the app's tests). Kotlin `main/` and `test/` =
`ringkit/src/{main,test}/kotlin/io/github/opencircuit/ringkit/`. The `:store` module's
source sets `commonMain/`, `jvmMain/`, `androidMain/`, `jvmTest/` and `androidDeviceTest/` =
`store/src/<set>/kotlin/io/github/opencircuit/store/`. The `:ble` module's source sets are
written `ble/<set>/` = `ble/src/<set>/kotlin/io/github/opencircuit/ble/` (`commonMain`,
`androidMain`, `jvmTestFixtures` — the test doubles other modules' tests use — and `jvmTest`).

One watermark per metric, keyed by `MetricKind.rawValue`. `isNew` means strictly newer; `advance` never moves backward; `selectNew` sorts by start and advances; `selectNewStaged` works on an independent copy and never moves the cursor it is called on, so a caller adopts the advanced cursor only after its save succeeds; `advancedKinds` lists moved metrics in `MetricKind` order. Every seconds value these sections print is the difference of two `Date` doubles, as Swift computes it (D-61). E3: `SleepEdgeProvenanceRow` (`:354-427`, both initializers, `DURATION_BASIS_RECORDED` / `DURATION_BASIS_EDITED`), unchanged since, because a sleep-confidence test builds the row to prove the cut travels from the assessment into it (D-64). E5b's second slice — schema 2 whole: `SleepRow` (`:46`, its defaulted fields defaulted), `DailyRow`, `StepSampleRow`, `NapRow`, `DaytimeTemperatureRow`, `HistorySyncEvidenceRow` (`:81-150`) and the `EpochArchiveRow` type (`:153`, written by a later slice); Swift `Int` fields are `Long` (Swift's `Int` is 64-bit; no in-module caller passes a 32-bit count, and a ported type's `Int` count is widened with `toLong()` at the call site), dates `Instant`, doubles compared by IEEE `==` as Swift's synthesized `Equatable`, every list copied in and read-only out — the evidence row holds copies of the `HistoryChannelTrace`s it is given and hands out fresh copies on every read, the archive row a copy of its coverage report; `sleepCSV`, `dailyCSV` (`:583`, `:604`) take the zone their `night` / `day` label prints in, `stepSamplesCSV`, `napsCSV`, `daytimeTemperatureCSV`, `historySyncEvidenceCSV` (`:613-680`) print UTC as upstream, `%.4f` / `%.2f` through `swiftFixed`, the channel summary's `:4d=` / `:sport=` each only when that counter is present; `sessionID` and `dayStamp` (`:432`, `:441`) take the zone; `toJSON` takes upstream's row parameters (`sleep` and `daily` required as upstream, the rest defaulted) and writes every schema-2 section through one shared `sleepJSON` (`:923`), explicit nulls where upstream writes `NSNull` (`jsonOrNull`, `:526-532`). The CSV field escaper tests every trigger per character (D-134). E5b's first slice: `schemaVersion` (`SCHEMA_VERSION`), `SampleRow` (Swift's `Equatable`: the value by IEEE `==`), the CSV field escaper (`csvField`, internal as upstream), `plainNumber` (`%.0f` through `swiftFixed` when whole, else Swift's `String(Double)`), `csvLine`, `samplesCSV`, and `toJSON` writing `schemaVersion`, `exportedAt`, `samples`, `provenance`, `units` (from `MetricKind.unit` and upstream's fixed list) and `notes` (copied from the pinned source's literals mechanically), with the v2 sections written empty until the second slice ported their rows. `JSONSerialization` (`[.prettyPrinted, .sortedKeys]`), `ISO8601DateFormatter`, `DateFormatter("yyyy-MM-dd")` and Swift's `String(Double)` are reproduced byte for byte in `FoundationText` and `ExportJson`, from behaviour measured on the pinned toolchain: the millisecond is `floor((d + 978307200) * 1000 + 0.5)` in double arithmetic on the `Date`'s own double (an `Instant` is first taken to the nearest double of its seconds since 2001), `String(Double)` switches to exponent form above 2^53 and below 1e-4, and JSON keys sort in Foundation's order (digit runs by value, a measured punctuation rank, letters case-folded, then the first case or leading-zero difference), not by code point. Strings are decoded as strict UTF-8: invalid bytes leave the field unset, as upstream's `String(bytes:encoding:.utf8)` does (Kotlin's `String(bytes)` would substitute U+FFFD). A new `SessionToken` per connection; the link ignores a callback carrying any other token, including every callback of an older connection to the same ring (an operation's answer, a notification, a disconnect), and a data frame of a closed connection never marks the new one authenticated. One link instance is reused across connections; each connection starts from scratch. |
| `A/BLE/RingSession.swift` `:952-1028` (`invalidate()`: the teardown contract; the delegate detached only if still ours, `:1027`) | `b1c2fdd` | `ble/commonMain/LinkCore.kt`, `ble/commonMain/LinkTeardown.kt` | `ble/jvmTest/DisconnectMidOpTest.kt`, `ble/jvmTest/StaleCallbackTest.kt` | E7 | REDESIGNED → `:ble/LinkCore.kt`. Ending a connection fails the operation in flight (`TIMED_OUT`, `GATT_ERROR` or `LINK_LOST`, as the cause was) and every write waiting behind it (`LINK_LOST`), stops the connection's timers, closes it exactly once and retires its token; no callback follows a close on Android, so nothing waits for one. A connection that had connected publishes one `LinkTeardown` on `RingLink.teardowns`: `LINK_DROPPED` for a failure, a drop or Bluetooth turning off, `USER_DISCONNECTED` for `disconnect()`, which also cancels any scheduled reconnect, and for the end of the link itself (its scope cancelled, or `close()` on the Android link; `ble/jvmTest/LinkCloseTest.kt`). Frames still waiting in the connection's buffer at teardown are dropped and counted in the teardown's `undeliveredFrames` (D-189), so a frame that was acknowledged and then lost is visible, never silent. |

Every failure or drop schedules the next attempt to the ring's address, never a scan, after `ReconnectBackoff.delay(attempt, inBackground = false)`: 1 s, 5 s, then 30 s, shown as `LinkState.Reconnecting(attempt, delay)`. The count is upstream's `reconnectAttempts`, reset to 0 by the same one-time check: 6 s after a connection connected, if it is still up and has delivered a frame (any notification, as upstream's `lastFrameAt`); a frame after the check does not reopen it. Direct first, then a standing connection (D-186). The background cap stays unused until background work exists. |

Bluetooth turning off (or reported turning off) fails the operation in flight with `LINK_LOST`, closes the connection, cancels a scheduled reconnect and shows `LinkState.BluetoothOff`; nothing is tried while it is off. Turning on (not "turning on") connects directly at once when a connection is wanted, else the link is `Idle`. A `connect()` while Bluetooth is off waits for it to come on. The Android adapter-state receiver reports each change; the link is told the adapter's state when it is built (it takes it to be on until told). |

| — (Android adapter) | — | `ble/androidMain/AndroidGattPort.kt`, `ble/androidMain/AndroidRingLink.kt`, `ble/androidMain/AndroidLinkBroadcasts.kt`, `ble/androidMain/AndroidScanPort.kt`, `ble/androidMain/AndroidRingScanner.kt` | — (compiled by `build`, not run on the JVM) | E7 | `GattPort` over `BluetoothGatt`: one framework call per method, API 33 forms (`writeCharacteristic(c, value, WRITE_TYPE_DEFAULT)`, `writeDescriptor(d, value)`, the value-carrying read and notification callbacks), `connectGatt(…, TRANSPORT_LE)`, the device rebuilt by address and type with `getRemoteLeDevice`. Callbacks only copy into a `GattEvent`; a revoked permission (`SecurityException`) refuses the operation. `RingLink(context, ring)` also registers, before the first `connect()` and with `RECEIVER_NOT_EXPORTED`, the adapter-state receiver (`ACTION_STATE_CHANGED`) and the bond receiver (`ACTION_BOND_STATE_CHANGED`, the link's ring only), tells the link the adapter's state when it is built, and unregisters both when the link's scope ends. `RingScanner(context)` scans with `BluetoothLeScanner`: one unfiltered low-latency scan per collection, each result copied into a report (address, raw `ScanRecord` bytes, name, service UUIDs, signal strength, and the address type Android reports on Android 15 and later). No hidden, system-only or reflective call; the reason Android gives for a lost bond is not public and is not read. The library manifest declares no permission: the app declares them. |

## Deviations & deferrals

### Index of every row

One line per row of the table below, in order: number · title · owning epic — where the work lives now for an open row, the epic that made it for a done one (rows D-1 to D-13 were raised in E1, D-14 to D-44 in E2, D-45 to D-70 in E3, D-71 to D-104 in E4, D-105 to D-130 in E5a, D-131 to D-136 in E5b, D-137 to D-159 in E6a, D-160 to D-182 in E6b, D-183 onwards in E7, D-201 to D-229 unused, D-230 onwards in E8). 254 rows.

- **D-249** · DEVIATION (addition): a scan Android refuses or ends with an error shows "Couldn't search for rings" with the reason and Search again · E8 (done)
- **D-250** · DEVIATION (addition): a chosen ring is paired through Android's companion-device sheet before the first bond, explained first, and the sheet's result code decides · E8 (done)
- **D-251** · DEVIATION: Stop reconnecting forgets the ring, removes its companion-device associations and retires its link; Cancel and Disconnect keep it remembered · E8 (done)
- **D-252** · DEVIATION: one link per ring, built once and kept; a different ring retires the old link first · E8 (done)
- **D-253** · DEVIATION (addition): a Connection details card in every build, with a Copy that masks the address and the ring's name · E8 (done)
- **D-254** · DEVIATION (addition): after a cancelled pairing sheet the user can pair without it, recorded as their choice · E8 (done)

A Kotlin class is a shared reference, so a `var` would let one holder change another's snapshot, and an in-place change never emits through a `StateFlow`. `copy(...)` keeps upstream's value semantics. Found by the Phase 3.5 reviewer fan-out. | — (done). E7 (`:ble`) accumulates DIS reads with `copy(...)`. |
| D-13 | **DEVIATION: Swift's value-type guarantees are spelled out.** `Frame.Parsed` throws `IllegalArgumentException` for an `opcode` or `trailer` outside 0–255, copies `body` on construction and returns a fresh copy on every read. `SportFrame.Sample` throws for a `cursor` outside 0–0xFFFFFFFF. | Swift's `UInt8` / `UInt32` made those values impossible, and its `[UInt8]` copies on assignment. A shared, writable Kotlin `ByteArray` let one holder change another's frame and move its `hashCode` (the same class as D-12). Every value upstream can produce is accepted unchanged. Reviewed and deliberately NOT changed, because each matches upstream and a check would reject input upstream accepts: `QuantitySample` / `SleepSegment` accept `end` before `start` and a NaN `value` (a throwing constructor could drop a segment, against D-10); `SkinTemperature`, `CaseBattery` and `Sample.hr`/`steps` carry no range check (their decoders already enforce the bands); `DeviceStatus` stays functions over a raw frame; `LiveHR.settled` does not re-filter (pinned by the ported settling tests); `DecodeAnomaly.hasSustainedTemperatureAnomaly` treats NaN as in-band and does not validate its parameters (documented in its KDoc; its only producer cannot emit NaN). Found by the Phase 5.5 reviewer fan-out. | — (done; `test/PortedTypeGuardTest.kt`) |
| D-14 | **DEVIATION: `SyncCursor` and `EpochSyncSession` are classes with `copy()`.** Upstream both are Swift `mutating struct`s, so every assignment was an independent copy. Here each is a mutable class owned by one drain: sharing the reference shares the state, and `copy()` gives a fully independent snapshot (a session's buffered pages are copied too). `SyncCursor.selectNewStaged` returns a `Staged(fresh, advanced)` data class instead of a tuple; it destructures the same way. The ported `advancedKinds` test writes `base.copy()` where upstream writes `var moved = base`. | The watermark and the page buffer change in place on every page of a drain. Keeping them mutable keeps upstream's call shape; `copy()` keeps its value semantics wherever a caller needs a snapshot, and `selectNewStaged` still never moves the cursor it is called on. | — (done; `test/HistoryTypeGuardTest.kt`) |

An instant `java.time` cannot place in a zone (the last year of `Instant`'s range) has no night key (`SleepNightKey.night` returns null where upstream always returns a date), never ends in the wake window, gives no morning wake, no credit and no move; a STORED key that cannot be placed is moved to the real key, as upstream moves it. | D-68 | **DEVIATION (owner decision): this morning's wake in the days after a spring-forward clock change.** Before the nearest sleep window's bedtime, upstream's `MissedNight.morningWake` steps `now` back a fixed 24 hours and takes the nearest window again. Kotlin steps back one calendar day from the nearest window's wake and places the wake on that day by the same rule as every other wake (the day's start plus the schedule's minutes). It does not step `now` itself back a calendar day: on a fall-back evening that is 25 hours and would land on yesterday's wake. The two rules differ only in the 56 hours after a change that loses time, in two ways. (1) A stale wake: on the evening of the changed day, and again on the evening of the day after, 24 hours back lands where an older wake is the nearer one, so upstream answers yesterday's wake (measured at 10-minute resolution for a 22:30–06:30 schedule: New York 2026-03-08 19:10–19:30 and 2026-03-09 18:40–19:00, London 2026-03-29 and 2026-03-30 and St John's 2026-03-08 and 2026-03-09 likewise, Chatham 2026-09-27 19:05–19:25 and 2026-09-28 18:35–18:55, Lord Howe 2026-10-04 18:50–19:00 and 2026-10-05 18:40; Santiago, Havana and Beirut on the day after only, 18:40–19:30). In that window a sync made yesterday evening counts as "after this morning's wake", so the missed-night banner can read MISSING where "not synced yet" was meant. (2) A wake an hour late: where the lost hour is the one after midnight (Santiago, Havana, Beirut), the changed day starts at 01:00 and upstream builds the next day's wake from that start, so on the evening of the day after the change (19:40–22:20 for the same schedule) it answers 07:30 for a 06:30 wake, although at noon the same day it answered 06:30; a sync made at 07:00 is then after the wake at noon and before it in the evening. Kotlin answers the scheduled wake all day. Sweep against upstream's rule, every 10 minutes across ±72 h of every 2026 transition in eleven zones with ten schedules: 816 of 190 300 answers change — 369 where upstream's was stale (Kotlin's is later), 447 where upstream's was an hour late (Kotlin's is earlier; the three midnight-gap zones only). Every changed answer is a wake the sleep window itself gives and has already passed; no fall-back or fixed-offset point changes. Measured over the whole of 2026 (5 781 600 points): the same 816 and nothing else. At one-minute resolution: 8 343 of 1 901 020. The owner chose the calendar-day step over keeping upstream's answer; the first measurement behind that choice covered only ±30 h (109 of 79 420, the changed day's evening alone), and the wider sweep replaced it before this port was merged. | The banner's wording on those evenings; a short, rare window, but the wrong one tells the user a night is missing, or changes its mind during the day. | — (done; `test/MissedNightHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-69 | **DEVIATION (owner decision): night selection stops at a declined bridge.** In `latestNightRecords`' backward cluster chain, upstream skips a bridge the observed-gap guard declines (`continue`) and keeps walking back. So a block that lies behind an UNOBSERVED hole further back can still be absorbed past the declined one, and the window then spans the declined block too, pulling its records back into the night — the very records the guard had just ruled out (measured on the pinned build: a 4 h night with a short block behind a fully observed awake hour and another short block behind an empty 2 h hole returns 180 records from the earliest block's start, identical to the guard switched off). Upstream's own comment calls this case untested and `break` "arguably cleaner". Kotlin ends the chain at the first declined bridge: that archive returns the 4 h night alone, 106 records. A bridge that is not declined chains exactly as before, and with the guard off nothing changes. Every ported upstream selection vector passes unchanged; in the Swift differential the 30 `sel` lines of the 10 `leapfrog` archives (UTC; shipped defaults, re-anchor off, morning absorb off) move, and no other line does (listed in `SleepDifferentialTest`'s deliberate-divergence list). The owner chose this over keeping upstream's walk. | A guard that declines a bridge should not be undone by the same walk one step later; the night it returns is the one the guard meant. | — (done; `test/NightSelectionHazardTest.kt`, `test/SleepDifferentialTest.kt`) |
| D-70 | **DEVIATION (owner decision): a duplicated record counts once in sleep detection, staging, night selection and naps.** Upstream reads every copy of a record. The detector's stillness window counts samples, so with every record twice the main block widened by 90 s at each end (measured: 1781351506..1781383696 against the single night's 1781351596..1781383606) and a nap likewise (1781547690..1781551080 against 1781547780..1781550990); staging staged the doubled timeline (6 segments over a wider in-bed span); `latestNightRecords` returned both copies (144 of 72); and `bridgeIsDeclined` counted a time once per copy, so a half-observed awake hour with every record doubled read as complete and was declined. Kotlin reads each counter once, keeping its FIRST copy in the caller's order and the input order otherwise, as a new list (the caller's is never changed): one helper, `BulkSleep.distinctRecords`, at the four record entries — `BulkSleep.mainSleep`, `BulkSleep.sleepSegments`, `BulkSleep.latestNightRecords` and `SleepStaging.classify` (so `stagedSegments` too) — plus `NapDetection.naps`; and `bridgeIsDeclined`, which takes sample times, counts each distinct time once. A doubled night, staging, selection and nap are exactly the single ones. Staging and naps are included because they read the block the detector finds: with detection alone deduplicated, a doubled night staged as neither upstream's nor the single night (measured), a mix. Metrics that take records directly (movement, stress, per-stage HR, averages, samples) are unchanged. `EpochArchive.merge` already deduplicates by counter before any of this; this is the same rule one layer down. No ported upstream vector changes, and no Swift-differential line moves (its inputs hold no duplicated counter). The owner chose this over keeping upstream's reading. | A record delivered twice (a re-drained page, a caller that skipped the archive merge) must not move a night's edges, change its stages or make an awake hour look observed. | — (done; `test/SleepDetectionHazardTest.kt`, `test/NightSelectionHazardTest.kt`, `test/SleepStagingHazardTest.kt`, `test/NapDetectionHazardTest.kt`) |

| D-76 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the resting-HR, exercise-minute and daily-estimate port.** (1) `RestingHR.dailyValues` takes a required `ZoneId` (upstream defaults to the device calendar): days start at `CalendarDay.startOfDay` and the next day is one calendar day later in wall-clock time, as Foundation's `date(byAdding: .day)` (Santiago's 6 September 2026, which has no midnight, runs 01:00 → 01:00). (3) Counts stay `Int`, upstream's 64-bit `Int` arithmetic is kept where it matters: credited steps, the residual and the qualifying-bpm sum are `Long` (a 32-bit sum wraps on three `Int.MAX_VALUE` step windows or two `Int.MAX_VALUE` readings); the max HR `220 − age` is computed in 64 bits and saturated to `Int.MAX_VALUE` — upstream's answer changes only for an age below −2 147 483 427, where both thresholds lie above every real heart rate (measured: the same estimate). (4) `RestingHR.DailyValue`, `Calories.EnergyBucket` and `Calories.DailyEstimate` compare doubles by IEEE `==`, as Swift's synthesized `Equatable` (NaN unequal to itself, −0.0 equal to 0.0); `DailyEstimate.buckets` is copied in and read-only out; `StepWindow` and `ElevatedPiece` are data classes. (5) `lowestSustainedDetailed` returns a small value type in place of Swift's tuple. Also kept as upstream after measuring: a duplicated isolated spot read earns a full exercise epoch, and a day of duplicated spot reads passes the switched-off personalised baseline's spot-read guard (removing duplicates is the record merge's job, as D-73); a sustained window of 0, below 0 or NaN on a single reading gives a NaN resting HR (a caller constant; production uses 300 s); a sleep floor of 0 or below gives 0.0 when no reading falls in sleep; a step window that ends before it starts is credited whole at its start, a negative delta is skipped and steps below zero earn nothing. | Upstream's numbers for every input the API accepts, with no default calendar; one home per type. | — (done; `test/DailyEnergyGuardTest.kt`, `test/RestingHRHazardTest.kt`, `test/ExerciseMinutesHazardTest.kt`, `test/DailyEnergyHazardTest.kt`) |

Outside the width range `plan` writes nothing and returns the state it was given, and `seed` carries the whole legacy total as debt — upstream's own answer for a width of 0 or below. A bucket that starts more than 26 h after the day start is skipped, exactly as upstream skips one that starts before it. Upstream sizes its watermark array by any ordinal: measured on the pinned build, a 1 ms width puts a 01:00 bucket in slot 3.6 million, a 1e-300 s width traps on the `Int` conversion (in `ordinal` and `seed`), a bucket ten years out builds 350 401 marks, and one at 3e16 s fails to allocate 2.7e14 bytes; 2e9 s and ∞ fold the whole day into slot 0. `ordinal` itself keeps upstream's arithmetic, returns 64 bits and saturates where Swift traps. Nothing the daily estimate produces changes (production width 900 s, every bucket inside the 26 h span). | A stored or caller-supplied width, or one corrupt bucket, must not be able to crash the app or exhaust its memory. | — (done; `test/ActiveEnergyLedgerHazardTest.kt`, with a time limit that fails rather than hangs) |
| D-79 | **DEVIATION: the energy write ledger fails closed on a value it cannot read.** `ActiveEnergyLedger.plan` writes nothing and returns the state it was given when a bucket of the day has NaN, infinite or negative energy, the day's total is not finite, a stored watermark is NaN, infinite or negative, or the carry, the workout credit or the saved total is NaN or infinite. A negative carry, credit or saved total still counts as zero, as upstream (a credit goes negative when a workout is deleted). Upstream, measured: a watermark of −40 writes 65 kcal into a 40 kcal bucket (only the day-total clamp, and only with a correct saved total, stops more); −∞ writes 65 kcal and stores NaN; a NaN watermark strands its bucket and stays NaN; a NaN carry, credit or saved total counts as 0 and the whole day is written again — the debt or the saved total that could not be read is forgotten; a +∞ bucket plans an infinite write; −10 kcal in one bucket cuts another bucket's write; two 1e308 buckets plan an infinite day. The state a plan hands back from readable input is always readable, so the rule never stops a valid day (a sweep of 300 seeded days, 12 committed flushes each). The vitals differential lists the 20 lines this moves (five unreadable states on each of its hostile days). | The health store sums active energy, so an over-write is permanent; one corrupt record stopping that day's writes is an under-write the next day heals. | — (done; `test/ActiveEnergyLedgerHazardTest.kt`, `test/VitalsDifferentialTest.kt`) |

A NaN legacy total is read as covering the day — upstream's own answer for an infinite one: every bucket full and infinite debt, so nothing more is written that day — where upstream reads it as 0 and the next plan writes the whole day again (measured: 65 kcal). When a bucket of the day has NaN, infinite or negative energy, nothing is placed and the whole legacy total is carried as debt (upstream: a NaN mark and NaN carry; a mark of −10 that grows the carry). Kept as upstream after measuring: a stored watermark list longer than the day nets its positive tail into debt and keeps its length, a shorter one is extended with zeros; buckets sharing a slot add up in `plan`; unsorted buckets are sorted; a bucket that ends before it starts, or has not begun at `now`, stays owed; a NaN minimum write never writes; NaN, negative or subnormal energy does not widen a window, and ∞ or huge energy starts it at the day start; an anchor at `now` widens back over time the last window covered (placement only — the amount comes from the ledger). | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; one home per type. | — (done; `test/EnergyLedgerGuardTest.kt`, `test/ActiveEnergyLedgerHazardTest.kt`, `test/ActiveEnergyWindowHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-82 | **DEVIATION: the energy ledger sums the day's total in ascending bucket order.** `ActiveEnergyLedger.plan` adds the day's per-bucket energy in ascending ordinal, where upstream adds a dictionary's values (`ActiveEnergyLedger.swift:169`) in an order Swift seeds per process — so upstream's own last bit is not fixed from one run to the next (three runs of the differential generator gave three different golden files). The total only bounds the writes (the day-total backstop), so a plan can differ from upstream's in the last bit of a clamped write: 172 of 27 804 differential doubles, largest difference 3.4e-13; with upstream's sum taken in ascending ordinal (a probe build) every double matches bit for bit. In the port: unreadable prior days and nights are dropped before the trailing window (fewer than the minimum readable → no baseline); an unreadable today is not judged (`classify` gives upstream's own no-baseline answer: normal, no baseline, delta 0); an unreadable offset or resting HR is absent (`suspectedFever` false, `report` no temperature signal); readings with an unreadable temperature are dropped before the count floor and the coverage (`coverage` itself reads only times); `anomalyFlags` raises nothing for an unreadable tonight and treats an unreadable baseline or previous night as absent; `report` keeps the baseline but gives an unreadable tonight no offset, no band and no flags; `VitalsBaseline.tempSeverity` and `SkinTempBaseline.deviationBand` return null for a NaN offset (an infinite one keeps its band). Finite input takes exactly upstream's path: inserting unreadable values anywhere into 500 seeded finite series leaves the stats bit-identical. | A stored day or night that cannot be read is not a day judged normal, and an infinity is not an alarm. | — (done; `test/RobustBaselineHazardTest.kt`, `test/VitalsBaselineHazardTest.kt`, `test/SkinTempBaselineHazardTest.kt`, `test/VitalsDifferentialTest.kt` — 104 listed lines) |

| D-91 | **DEVIATION: goal settings are read through a caller-supplied lookup, with Foundation's number bridging; an integer beyond 32 bits reads as missing.** Upstream reads `UserDefaults.standard` with `object(forKey:) as? Int` / `as? Double`. `:ringkit` has no settings store, so `GoalDefaults.stepsGoal` / `sleepGoalMinutes` and `GoalHistory.Goals.fromDefaults` take a `GoalSettings` (`fun interface GoalSettings { fun value(key: String): Any? }`) the app supplies over its own store. Measured on the pinned build with a real suite (36 stored values), `as? Int` takes any number whose value is whole (9000.0 → 9000, −0.0 → 0, a float 9000 → 9000, `true` → 1, `false` → 0) and `as? Double` any number the double holds exactly (an integer → x.0, the float 0.1 → 0.10000000149011612, NaN, ∞); a fractional value read as an integer, NaN or ∞ read as an integer, 2^53 + 1 read as a double, a string (even "9000"), a list, a map, a date or data read as missing, and the default answers. The port applies that rule to Kotlin's boxed `Int`, `Long`, `Short`, `Byte`, `Double`, `Float` and `Boolean`; anything else is the wrong type. Bound: upstream's integers are 64-bit, so a stored 2^31 or 2^40 is returned as the goal; a Kotlin goal is an `Int`, and a value it cannot hold reads as missing (the default) rather than a wrapped or saturated goal nobody set. A lookup that throws is not caught. | No ambient settings store; every value in the `Int` range reads exactly as iOS reads it. | — (done; `test/GoalDefaultsHazardTest.kt`, `test/GoalUnitGuardTest.kt`) |
| D-92 | **DEVIATION: default units come from the locale passed in — US and Liberia get °F and miles; the locale's unit preference and region override count; a locale with no region is metric.** Upstream reads `Locale.current.measurementSystem == .us`. Measured on this Mac with the pinned build's Foundation across all 292 regions: only US and LR use the US system — Myanmar uses the UK system, so it gets °C and km (an older list of US-system regions included Myanmar; the port follows the measurement). Upstream also honours the locale's own unit preference (`-u-ms-metric` / `ussystem` / `uksystem`) before a region override (`-u-rg-xxzzzz`) before the region, and so does the port. The JDK has no measurement-system data, so the rule is that two-region table plus the two keywords. A locale with no region (`en`, the root locale) reads metric: Foundation fills a region in from the language (`en` → US), and `java.util.Locale` has no such table; a device locale carries a region. | No ambient locale; the same answer as iOS for every locale with a region. | — (done; `test/LocaleDefaultUnitsTest.kt`, `test/UnitsFormatterHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-93 | **DEVIATION (bound): one shared fixed-decimal formatter reproduces Foundation's `%.Nf` and never crashes.** The three `UnitsFormatter` number shapes (`UnitPreferences.swift:99`, `:108`, `:115`) and the archive diagnostics' gap hours use `swiftFixed` in `SwiftNumerics.kt`. Measured on the pinned build: the exact binary value is rounded ties to even (0.25 → "0.2", 0.125 → "0.12", 2.5 → "2"; 0.35 → "0.3", as it sits below its tie); NaN prints "nan" whatever its sign and even when a sign is forced; infinities print "inf", "+inf", "-inf"; −0.0, and a negative value that rounds to zero, print "-0.0"; large values print their whole decimal expansion; a negative `fractionDigits` builds "%.-Nf", which Foundation reads as no decimals left-justified in a field N wide; the number is cut to its first 510 characters; and once \|`fractionDigits`\| reaches 2^31 − 512 upstream crashes with a segmentation fault. The port writes the same text for every `Int` width, computing at most 1 100 decimals (a double's expansion has at most 1 074, and only 510 characters are kept), so a huge width allocates nothing and the crashing widths get the capped text. Java's `String.format` is not used: it rounds half up and reads the default locale. The diagnostics' `oneDecimal` now delegates; its text is unchanged for every value the report can print (non-negative gap hours — swept over 3.1 million values), and differs only for −0.0, negatives that round to zero and non-finite values, now as upstream's `%.1f`. | Byte-identical strings for every width upstream answers; no crash where upstream crashes; one home for the rule. | — (done; `test/UnitsFormatterHazardTest.kt`, `test/VitalsDifferentialTest.kt` — 627 format lines byte-identical) |

The credit map iterates oldest day first. | No wrapped (negative) sleep, no throw on a far timestamp, no quadratic walk over a long history. | — (done; `test/GoalHistoryHazardTest.kt`, `test/GoalUnitGuardTest.kt`) |

| D-98 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the goals and units, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) No ambient clock, zone, calendar or store: `isWeekend`, `stepsGoal`, `sleepGoalMinutes`, `Goals.stepGoal` / `sleepGoalMinutes`, `build`, `sleepCreditByDay` and `summarize` take a required `Instant` / `ZoneId` / `GoalSettings`; `Goals.fromDefaults(settings)` keeps upstream's name. The weekend is Saturday or Sunday in the zone: upstream asks the device calendar for weekday 1 or 7, and all sixteen Foundation calendars (Gregorian, Buddhist, Chinese, Coptic, both Ethiopic, Hebrew, Indian, four Islamic, ISO 8601, Japanese, Persian, Republic of China) number weekdays the same way for every first weekday (measured in five zones, hourly over two weeks), so no calendar parameter is taken. An instant no zone can place is a workday. | D-107 | **DEVIATION (improvement): sleep regularity scores every night at one clock minute exactly 100.** Bedtimes that are all equal modulo 1440 have no spread. Here such a list scores 100; every other list takes upstream's formula unchanged (any other list's R sits well below 1, where an ulp barely moves the score). Found by the engine differential: the JVM's `StrictMath` differs from the platform libm by one ulp at 128 of the 1 440 bedtime angles, so a plain port flipped 156 of the 4 320 answers, both ways. | The most regular sleeper scoring 99 by the minute of her bedtime is a rounding artefact; the exact answer also takes the port off the platform's libm at the one spot where the last bit decides. (4) Kept difference, measured on the pinned build: an instant `java.time` cannot place in the zone (the first and last year of `Instant`'s range) raises no sedentary or bedtime reminder and nothing throws, where Foundation still reads a time of day there; far instants it can place (to 1e13 s, back to Foundation's distant past) agree with upstream's time of day. | D-117 | **DEVIATION (bound): the cycle predictor's roll-forward is bounded.** `predict` adds the average cycle to the predicted start one addition at a time, as upstream does, for up to 2²⁰ cycles — enough for every date Foundation represents (its distant future from 2026 is about 35 000 additions, measured; 1e12 s is 412 637) — and past that adds the remaining whole cycles in one step and finishes with upstream's loop. Upstream's loop always ends (the average is 21…45 days), but at `Instant`'s ends it would add about 1.3e10 times, for minutes. Past the bound the answer is still the first step at or after `now`; a step past `Instant`'s end saturates at `Instant.MAX`. | A clock no phone shows must not hang a prediction; every clock upstream answers in practice takes upstream's exact path. | — (done; `test/CyclePredictorHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-118 | **DEVIATION (improvement): the period mirror counts calendar days when a period starts on a day whose midnight the zone skips.** Where a clock change skips midnight itself (Santiago, Havana and Beirut in 2026), that day starts at 01:00. Upstream adds days to that 01:00 and counts whole days from it, so a period starting that day is mirrored one day short — measured on days 0 / 6 / 7 / 8 / 12: an open period 1, 6, 7, 8, 8 days (the cap reached a day late), with 10 days already covered 1, 6, 7, 8, 10, and a 5-day logged period 4 days for good. The port counts calendar days in the zone: 1, 7, 8, 8, 8 / 1, 7, 8, 9, 10 / 5, and a last day is the start of its calendar day. Every other start agrees with upstream, including starts whose span crosses the skipped midnight. | One day of a period the wearer logged never reached the health store; the cap and the anti-retraction floor keep their meaning. | — (done; `test/CyclePredictorHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-119 | **DEVIATION: the cycle predictor keeps Foundation's `Double`-seconds date arithmetic and converts to `Instant` once.** Upstream's `Date` is one `Double` of seconds since 2001, and `cycleStats` and `predict` subtract, add, compare and roll forward in those doubles, rounding at every step. The port reads each `Instant` as that double (whole seconds since 2001 plus nanoseconds / 1e9), does upstream's arithmetic, and converts each result to an `Instant` once, to the nearest nanosecond; at today's dates a double holds about a tenth of a microsecond, so the round trip loses nothing. Measured with the engine differential (28 histories, 440 values): every statistic and every predicted date is bit-identical. The same maths in `Instant` nanoseconds misses 14 golden lines by more than 1e-6 s — up to 0.0226 s after the roll-forward to Foundation's distant future, and one prediction a whole cycle late where the clock sits exactly on upstream's predicted start — and leaves 45 more dates not bit-identical. | The predicted days the wearer sees, and the cycle the roll-forward lands on, match iOS's to the bit. | — (done; `test/CyclePredictorHazardTest.kt`, `test/EngineDifferentialTest.kt`) |
| D-120 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the cycle predictor, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `CyclePredictor` is an object; the constants are `const val`s in UPPER_SNAKE; upstream's `(night: Date, offsetC: Double)` tuple is `SkinTempNight`. `PeriodEntry` is a data class; `CycleStats`, `CyclePrediction` and `SkinTempNight` are immutable and compare their doubles by IEEE `==`, as Swift's synthesized `Equatable` does; lists are read, never kept or reordered. (2) No ambient zone or clock: the nine functions upstream defaulted to `Calendar.current` take a required `ZoneId`; `predict`'s `now` is required; `skinTempDeviations` and `alreadyCoveredDays` keep upstream's defaults. (3) A mirror day count is a `Long` (Swift's 64-bit `Int`); `alreadyCoveredDays` and `writtenSampleCount` are `Int`s (counts of tracked samples). (4) The day classifiers and mirror bounds work on calendar days (`LocalDate`) in the zone, which agree with Foundation's start-of-day comparisons and day counts across both 2026 New York clock changes (measured), except where midnight is skipped (D-118); `openPeriodAutoExtendLastDay` and `periodMirrorLastDay` return the start of that day. (5) Kept difference, measured: an instant `java.time` cannot place in the zone (`Instant`'s first and last year) is no day — never logged, predicted or ovulation; no last day (`null`), 0 days, never up to date, never capped — where Foundation still reads a day; nothing throws. Foundation's own distant dates agree (1 460 973 days between them). (6) Kept as upstream after measuring: the same skin-temperature night given twice counts twice (the caller passes one value per night); a NaN offset never counts and +∞ does; the ±3-day window is closed; unsorted, duplicated, backwards and overlapping histories; tied starts keep their given order and their durations sum in it; covered-day counts at the `Int` ends (Foundation never answers "no date", so the floor reaches today); today before the start and an end before the start mirror nothing. | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; the zone and the clock are the caller's. | — (done; `test/CyclePredictorGuardTest.kt`, `test/CyclePredictorHazardTest.kt`, `test/CyclePredictorClockChangeTest.kt`) |
| D-121 | **DEVIATION (bound): a detected-workout retention upstream cannot read is bounded.** (1) `CursorSpan.migratedFromLegacyCursor` widens a cursor by `UInt32(max(retention, 0))`, which traps upstream on NaN, an infinity or a value of 2³² or more (measured: "Double value cannot be converted to UInt32"). The port converts as upstream's own `UInt32(clamping:)` does two lines later, after truncation: NaN widens nothing, anything past the top widens by 0xFFFFFFFF. Every other retention gives upstream's answer (measured: −∞ and −5 → no widening, 1.9 → 1, 4 294 967 295.9 → the end wraps to one before the cursor), and the end still wraps past 0xFFFFFFFF, masked to 32 bits as upstream's `&+` wraps (0xFFFFFF00 + 48 h → 172 544). (2) `prunedToRelevant` with a NaN retention compares every span against a NaN horizon upstream and drops them all (measured), so a caller saving the result would erase the dismissed and announced ledger and every reviewed bout would come back. The port keeps every span — the answer upstream's own `AutomaticWorkoutInbox.rebuild` gives on a NaN retention, which keeps every sample (measured: 140 of 140). Every other retention gives upstream's answer. | No retention crashes the inbox's bookkeeping or erases what the wearer already dismissed; the app passes the 48 h constant, so its answers are upstream's. | — (done; `test/WorkoutHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-122 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the workout session and automatic workout detection, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `HRZoneClassifier`, `WorkoutHRBackfill`, `WorkoutBufferedSportFill`, `HistoricalSportFrame`, `AutomaticWorkoutDetector`, `AutomaticWorkoutInbox`, `AutomaticWorkoutAnnouncement` and `AutomaticWorkoutConfirmation` are objects; constants are `const val`s in UPPER_SNAKE (`TimeInterval`s as `Double` seconds). `WorkoutSportType` (10), `HRZone` (raw `Int`) and `SuggestedKind` keep upstream's raw names in declaration order, with `fromRawValue`; `systemImageName` keeps upstream's SF Symbols names verbatim for the app to map. `WorkoutZoneBreakdown`, `WorkoutSummary` and `CursorSpan` are data classes, comparing their doubles by IEEE `==` as Swift's synthesized `Equatable` does; `Sample`, `Candidate`, `Fill`, `State` and `Prepared` copy their lists and bytes in and read them out read-only, and `Fill` is built only by `fill` (upstream's memberwise initializer is internal). (2) `WorkoutSessionAggregator` was already a reference type upstream; it is a mutable class owned by one live workout, with `copy()` for an independent snapshot (samples and the live high-water mark included); `finalize` does not consume it, and `collectedSamples` is a snapshot. (3) Ring cursors (Swift `UInt32`) are `Long`s required in 0…0xFFFFFFFF wherever a `Sample` or `CursorSpan` is built; the window span clamps to that range; the migration's wrap is masked (D-121). A record's step count is an `Int` (a decoded byte, 0…255); every sum of steps — `Candidate.steps`, `Fill.steps`, `WorkoutSummary.steps` and `finalize`'s `steps` — is a `Long` (Swift's 64-bit `Int`), and the bpm sum behind an average is a `Long` whose quotient truncates toward zero, as Swift's `/` does. (4) `AutomaticWorkoutInbox.rebuild` takes a required `now` (upstream defaulted to the device clock); the other defaults are upstream's settings. Where upstream de-duplicates through a Dictionary (`merge`, `detect`, `rebuild`), the port uses an insertion-ordered map and sorts its values on their unique keys, so no iteration order reaches a result; the held-zone sort is stable, as Swift's. (5) Kept differences (the exact-elapsed-time rule): elapsed times are measured exactly between instants, so a window edge 100 ns before a whole second is the previous cursor and a bout 24 h and 1 ns old is past the announcement age, where upstream's `Date` doubles cannot hold the difference. (6) Kept as upstream after measuring: a NaN coverage floor finds no candidate and a NaN hold cap holds every gap uncapped (Swift's `min` / `max` let NaN through); a NaN or infinite inbox retention keeps every sample; a record repeated in the buffered fill is used twice and a stored record's HR is not re-validated (the caller passes the inbox's de-duplicated records); a hand-built breakdown's fractions (−0.5 / 1.5 with a negative field, ∞ / ∞ → NaN, a NaN total → 0); the energy estimate keeps the first of `[HR, distance]` unless the later is greater (a NaN distance estimate never displaces a number); any stored age bounds max HR to 1…219; a hand-built candidate with its end before its start confirms with a negative duration and 0 kcal. | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; the clock is the caller's, and no 32-bit sum or shared buffer changes an answer. | — (done; `test/WorkoutHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-123 | **DEVIATION (bound): a stored workout-recovery snapshot holding a value no session writes is read as no snapshot.** `WorkoutSessionRecovery.decide` returns `NothingToRecover` — upstream's answer for a snapshot it cannot read — for a snapshot whose energy estimate is NaN, infinite or negative, whose reading count is negative, or whose average or maximum heart rate is negative. This is checked before the span refusals, as upstream reads its stored form before it decides. Upstream's own stored form cannot hold a non-finite energy (measured: `encoded()` is nil for NaN, +∞ and −∞, and a stored `"NaN"` does not decode), so end to end upstream never offers one; it does decode and offer negative counts, energy and heart rates as they are (measured: −3 readings, −5 kcal, −104 / −1 bpm). No session writes any of these: the live estimate is a high-water mark starting at 0, and counts and heart rates are never negative. Every value a session writes (−0.0, 0 and 1e308 kcal, no energy, counts 0 … `Int.MAX_VALUE`, heart rate 0) is offered exactly as upstream offers it. | The snapshot is a stored value from a previous process; offering a corrupt one would write its values to the health store, which a later launch cannot take back. Failing closed as "cannot read" keeps upstream's two refusals and their stored names unchanged. | — (done; `test/WorkoutHRGateAndRecoveryHazardTest.kt`) |

| D-125 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the live-HR gate and the workout recovery decision, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `WorkoutHRGate` and `WorkoutSessionRecovery` are objects; `DEFAULT_MAX_AGE` is a `const val` of `Double` seconds, and the freshness window is `Double` seconds whose NaN and infinite values keep upstream's meaning. (2) `WorkoutSessionSnapshot` and `RecoveredWorkout` are data classes (`val` + `copy`) comparing their doubles by IEEE `==`, as Swift's synthesized `Equatable` does; `WorkoutRecoveryRefusal` keeps upstream's two raw names in declaration order, with `fromRawValue` matching the exact string; `WorkoutRecoveryDecision` is a sealed interface — `NothingToRecover`, `Discard(refusal)`, `Offer(workout)` (upstream's associated values are unlabelled). (4) A reading count is an `Int` (Swift's 64-bit `Int`): a live session's count is a list size and always fits; a stored count past 2³¹ is the stored form's concern (D-124). (5) Kept differences (the exact-elapsed-time rule): a lock 6 s and 1 ns old is stale, a heartbeat 1 ns after the start is an observed span and one 1 ns after `now` ends in the future, where upstream's `Date` doubles cannot hold the nanosecond. (6) Kept as upstream after measuring: a lock dated after `now` records (its age is negative); a last recording in the future blocks every real lock until the clock passes it; a session start in the future admits nothing and no session start admits a pre-workout lock; a negative window admits only a lock at least that far ahead, NaN and −∞ admit nothing, +∞ any age; a snapshot both never observed and future-dated is "no observed span" (checked first); a recovered workout built by hand ending before it starts has a negative duration. | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; the clock is the caller's. | — (done; `test/WorkoutHRGateAndRecoveryHazardTest.kt`, `test/WorkoutGuardTest.kt`) |
| D-126 | **DEVIATION (improvement): an alarm time inside a clock change that is not a whole hour stays on its day.** The alarm's wall-clock time is resolved by one helper, `CalendarDay.atWallClock`, which reproduces Foundation's `date(bySettingHour:minute:second:of:)` as measured on the pinned build over every minute of both 2026 changing days in New York, Adelaide, Lord Howe and Santiago: a time the clock shows once is that instant; one it shows twice is the first; one it skips is the end of the gap (New York's 02:30 on 8 March → 03:00, where `java.time`'s own rule gives 03:30); an hour outside 0…23 or a minute outside 0…59 is no time; the answer depends only on the date. The one difference: Lord Howe moves its clock 30 minutes (02:00 → 02:30, 4 October 2026), and Foundation resolves 02:00–02:29 that day to the same time the NEXT day — so upstream's 02:15 alarm never sounds that morning (its most recent occurrence stays the day before's, its next is the day after's). The port resolves it to 02:30 that day, the end of the gap, as Foundation does for every whole-hour gap. | Upstream's own test asks that a time the clock skips resolve to a real instant "rather than vanishing for the day"; Foundation does so for whole-hour gaps and not for this one. Every other time in every zone is unchanged (swept over every gap and overlap of every zone the JVM knows in 2026–27). | — (done; `test/RingAlarmAndProximityHazardTest.kt`) |

The dial is now empty whenever the band is "searching" and stays within 0…1 (swept −2 000…2 000 and the `Int` ends). | — (done; `test/RingAlarmAndProximityHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-128 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the ring alarm and ring proximity, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `RingAlarm` is an immutable class (`val` + `copy`) whose weekday set is copied in and read-only (ascending); it compares `burstSpacing` by IEEE `==`, as Swift's synthesized `Equatable`; `hour`, `minute` and `burstCount` are `Int`s (a stored value past 32 bits is the stored form's concern, D-129). `RingAlarmDecision` is a sealed interface — `Fire(scheduled, lateBy)` (`lateBy` in `Double` seconds, compared by IEEE `==`), `Missed(scheduled)`, `Idle`. `RingAlarmBlock` keeps upstream's four raw names in declaration order with `fromRawValue` (exact match); it is used only by the app's alarm controller but is ported here. `RingAlarmSchedule` and `RingProximity` are objects; the constants are `const val`s in UPPER_SNAKE (`TimeInterval`s as `Double` seconds); `Band` is an enum (no raw names upstream) whose `label`s are verbatim. Swift's argument labels are dropped (`repeats(weekday)`, `mostRecentOccurrence(now, alarm, zone)`, `band(rssi)`). (2) No ambient zone or clock: the four rules upstream defaulted to the device's calendar (`warmUpTarget`, `mostRecentOccurrence`, `nextOccurrence`, `decide`) take a required `ZoneId`; `now` was already required; the warm-up window and the grace keep upstream's defaults. (3) Days are walked by calendar date in the zone; a weekday is Foundation's number (1 = Sunday); `lateBy` and the warm-up window are measured exactly between instants (the elapsed-time rule of the earlier slices). (4) `approximateMeters` uses `StrictMath.pow` (the engine differential: 18 of the distances are one ulp from upstream's platform `pow`, every text identical); `distanceText` rounds as Swift's `rounded()` and formats nothing through the default locale. (5) Kept difference: an instant `java.time` cannot place in the zone (`Instant`'s first and last year) has no occurrence and the decision is idle, where upstream's `Date` reaches it and its calendar answers; nothing throws. Foundation's distant dates and both sides of 1582's switch to the Julian calendar agree exactly (measured). (6) Kept as upstream after measuring: a weekday outside 1…7 is taken literally, so a set holding only such days never fires; NaN, negative and −∞ grace never fire (missed) and +∞ always fires; a NaN, non-positive or −∞ warm-up never arms, and +∞ arms on every next occurrence; a "last handled" stamp at or after the occurrence holds it, even one in the future; a NaN burst spacing stays NaN (upstream's stored form cannot hold one); the burst count clamps to 1…10 at every `Int`. | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; the zone and the clock are the caller's. | — (done; `test/RingAlarmAndProximityGuardTest.kt`, `test/RingAlarmAndProximityHazardTest.kt`) |

| D-130 | **DEVIATION: no main file reads an ambient clock, time zone, locale, preferences store or the environment — the audit covers the whole module.** The source audit reads every main file, listed from the source directory (113 at E5a's close), where it read a hand-kept list of the files E4 added or changed. Measured when it was widened: one ambient read outside that list — `Command.syncUpToNow` defaulted `now` to the device clock (`Instant.now()`, as upstream's `Date()`) — so `now` is now required; every caller already passed it. Five hits that are not ambient reads are excused by name, each with its reason: the debug `toString`s of `BulkRecord`, `EpochRecord.ActivityRecord` (and its hex helper) and `Frame.Parsed` format a literal in the default locale, but only with lowercase `%x` and `%s`, which java.util.Formatter never localizes — the audit pins the same ASCII text under Arabic, Devanagari, Thai and Persian digits and Turkish case rules. An exception that no longer matches exactly one hit fails the audit. Preferences-store reads join the rules. | The rule each port set for its own files, carried to every file, with no list to forget: the same inputs give the same answer on every machine, in every zone and language. | — (done; `test/NoAmbientEnvironmentTest.kt`) |
| D-131 | **KEPT DIFFERENCE: export dates before 1582-10-15 print proleptic Gregorian, and a fixed offset with seconds prints them.** (1) Foundation's `ISO8601DateFormatter` and `DateFormatter` switch to the Julian calendar before the Gregorian cutover (measured: −62 135 596 800 s prints `0001-01-03`); the port prints `java.time`'s proleptic Gregorian `0001-01-01`, and a year before 1 with a minus sign. (2) For a fixed zone with a seconds offset (`TimeZone(secondsFromGMT: 3661)`) Foundation prints `+01:01`, dropping the seconds, while it prints a region zone's seconds offset in full (Monrovia 1970: `-00:44:30`); the port prints any offset's seconds (`+01:01:01`). | Neither can reach the export on valid input: no ring record or stored row predates the ring's 2020s sync epoch, and a device zone is a region zone, whose offsets the two print alike. Reproducing the Julian calendar or the fixed-zone quirk would add code no valid input exercises. | — (done; `test/FoundationTextHazardTest.kt` pins the port's text and quotes Foundation's) |

The `meta`, `historySyncEvidence` and `epochArchive` sections hold no double. Done. |

(2) Byte parity for every sample-only export from the first slice, with the full parameter list arriving with its row types. | E5b: (2) closed in the second slice. In the fourth, `metadataCSV`, `sleepSessionsCSV` and `hypnogramCSV` take the zone their labels and offsets print in, and the metadata block's declared zone and offset are required, with `ExportMetadata.of(zone, exportedAt, …)` deriving both from that zone (declared offset = printed offset, `test/ExportEngineGuardTest.kt`). | D-136 | **KEPT FOR BYTE PARITY: twelve quantities of an edited night's `provenanceSummary` have no unit.** Upstream's `units` block promises a unit for every numeric field that expresses a quantity, and its test `testEveryNumericLeafInTheJSONHasAUnitOrIsAnExplicitCount` walks the JSON to hold it — but on a fully measured night, which writes no `provenanceSummary`. On a night with asserted time the summary writes `measuredAsleepSec`, `assertedOverMeasuredAsleepSec`, `assertedAsleepSec`, `coverageUnknownAsleepSec`, `measuredAwakeSec`, `assertedOverMeasuredAwakeSec`, `assertedAwakeSec`, `coverageUnknownAwakeSec`, `coveredInBedSec`, `coverageUnknownInBedSec`, `longestUnmeasuredGapSec` (seconds) and `measuredEfficiency` (a fraction), and `units` names none of them; nor are they on the test's allow-list of counts. The port writes upstream's `units` block unchanged. | Adding the units would change the `units` block of every JSON file and `units.csv`, so no export would match upstream's bytes any more; that trade is a decision, not a port detail. | E5b (kept, 2026-10-03: byte parity wins; the unit map is to be settled once for both platforms — reported upstream as a candidate fix, and revisited by the app-layer export epic): `test/ExportEngineGuardTest.kt` pins the gap to exactly these keys on a full export, so no other number can lose its unit unnoticed. |
| D-137 | **DEVIATION: the store keeps every instant as whole epoch milliseconds.** Upstream's SwiftData store keeps a `Date` (a `Double` of seconds, about 0.1 µs apart at today's dates). Every instant column of the Room store is an `INTEGER` of epoch milliseconds; writing cuts the sub-millisecond part toward the past (before 1970 too) and never rounds. The ingest makes the same cut on each sample's start and end before the sync cursor compares them, so a sample with sub-millisecond time is not seen as newer than its own stored cursor and stored again on every re-sync. | Ring times are whole seconds, so nothing the ring sends loses precision; an integer column is exact, sorts and indexes without floating-point ties, and a `java.time.Instant` carries nanoseconds that no reading has. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/StoredSampleColumnsTest.kt` (nanoseconds, before 1970) and `jvmTest/IngestTransactionTest.kt` (re-ingesting a sub-millisecond sample adds nothing). |
| D-138 | **NOT PORTED: upstream's store recovery paths that delete the store.** When the container fails to open, upstream's `resolveContainer` (`A/App.swift:557`) falls back to `wipeAndRecoverForeground` (`:582`), which deletes the store files and rebuilds an empty store, after `RollupBackup` (`:742`) exports the sleep and daily rollups (a file upstream never reads back), or, in the background, to an in-memory store (`:603`). Here `StoreFactory` opens the store at once and a failure reaches the caller with the file untouched; no source or build script under `android/` asks Room for a destructive migration fallback. **Not ported, named:** `AT/HeadacheStoreTests.swift` `:225` and `:273` (they test `RollupBackup`). | The wipe deletes every raw sample, cursor, step sample and daytime temperature, which the ring cannot send again; a store that does not open must never cost the user their history. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/StoreOpenTest.kt`, `jvmTest/NoDestructiveFallbackTest.kt`, `jvmTest/StoreMigrationTest.kt`. What the user sees on a failed open, and whether a background task falls back to memory, belong to the app's launch and background-sync epics. |
| D-139 | **NOT PORTED: upstream's schema version 8 (a device id on samples and cursors).** Upstream commits after the pinned one add a `SchemaV8` that tags stored samples and cursors with the device they came from (strap support). The store ports the pinned version 7 shape. | It came after the pin, for devices this port does not support; with one ring per store it changes nothing. | The multi-ring epic, as an ordinary schema migration with its migration test. |
| D-140 | **DEVIATION (bound): a NaN or infinite heart rate — or value of any kind — is dropped at ingest.** Upstream's plausibility check is `LiveHR.validBPM.contains(Int(s.value))` (`A/Store/LocalStore.swift:1042`). `Int(Double)` truncates toward zero, which the port keeps (29.9 is dropped, 220.9 kept — measured on Swift 6.3.2), but traps on NaN and ±infinity ("Double value cannot be converted to Int because it is either infinite or NaN", measured), so upstream's ingest crashes. Here such a heart rate is implausible and dropped, in the dry run and the write alike. A NaN or infinite value of any other kind is dropped too and counted as `invalidValueCount` in the dry run: SQLite binds NaN as NULL, which `stored_sample.value` refuses (measured: `NOT NULL constraint failed`), so one such sample failed the whole batch's transaction, its cursor with it, on every sync. | A crash at ingest would lose the whole batch and repeat on every sync; a value that is not a number is not a heart rate. Every finite value keeps upstream's outcome. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/IngestPlausibilityHazardTest.kt`. |
| D-141 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the store's open and ingest path, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `LocalStore` is a class over one `StoreDatabase`; every read and write is `suspend` (Room's DAOs are). (2) `ingest` takes `now` and `zone`, and `previewIngest` takes `now`, where upstream reads the clock and `Calendar.current`; the result is the same for the same clock and zone. (3) The store opens only through `StoreFactory`: the bundled SQLite driver on every target (the same SQLite 3.50.1, with the same compile options, as the JVM tests — checked on the emulator), opened at once. (4) Where upstream sorts by `start` alone and SwiftData leaves ties unspecified, reads break ties by insertion order. (5) `LaunchSnapshot` is not a type; its one read is `latestSample(HEART_RATE)`. (6) Kept as upstream: the plausibility edges (both inclusive), one stored-state lookup per cumulative kind per batch, a cumulative counter's day total restarting at local midnight inside a batch, cursor rows written only for the kinds that advanced, the health (`hk:`) and export (`export:`) watermark rows sharing the cursor table but never read as the ingest cursor, no uniqueness on `(kind, start)`, and a row of an unknown kind kept as stored. | Kotlin and Room idiom, and a store whose answers depend only on its arguments. | E6a (done): the tests named in the ported-files rows above. |
| D-142 | **DEVIATION (improvement): each one-time purge's latch is written in the purge's own transaction.** Upstream runs `purgeImplausibleHeartRate` and `purgeImplausibleTimestamps` once per install behind two `UserDefaults` flags, `store.purgedImplausibleHR.v1` and `store.purgedImplausibleTimestamps.v1`, set after the purge's save (`A/App.swift:624-633`, `:638-647`), so the flag and the purge are two commits. Here each flag is a `store_kv` row with the same key, read and written inside the purge's transaction: the latch is set exactly when the purge committed. A failed purge leaves no latch and runs again next launch; a failed latch write rolls the purge back. `purgeImplausibleHeartRateOnce` / `purgeImplausibleTimestampsOnce` return the count deleted, or null once latched; a latch row holding anything but `true` counts as unset (the purges are idempotent, so a rerun costs a scan, never data). | A latch and the work it records cannot disagree, and the store has one home for its state instead of two. On valid runs the outcome is upstream's: each purge runs once. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/RetentionAndRepairTest.kt` (latch write failing rolls back either purge — red under a latch written after the commit; purge failing writes no latch — red under a latch written first; second run skipped). |
| D-143 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the store's step, retention and repair paths, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) Every call that reads the clock or cuts days takes `now` / `zone` (`addDailySteps`, `todaySteps`, `pruneExpiredSamples`, the purges, `repairFutureSyncCursors`), where upstream reads `Date()` and `Calendar.current`; the prune's cutoff is `now.atZone(zone).minusDays(30)`, measured equal to Foundation's `date(byAdding: .day, value: -30)` on five cases including two DST gaps and a fall-back (Swift 6.3.2). (2) Step totals and deltas are `Long` (Swift's `Int` is 64 bits) and an overflowing total throws inside the transaction, as Swift's `+=` traps; upstream's `try?` on the day lookup (a failed read inserts a second row for the day) is not kept: a failed read fails the write. (3) Rollup reads return values (`DailySteps`, `StepSample`, `DaytimeTemperature`), not the stored rows. (4) The prune's three time columns are indexed. (5) `LaunchRepairs.run` runs every step whatever happened to the one before (as upstream's separate launch tasks) but returns each outcome instead of swallowing it; cancellation stops the run. (6) Kept as upstream: the prune deletes `time < cutoff` (a row at the cutoff stays) from samples, step deltas and daytime readings only; the heart-rate purge compares the stored `Double` (`value < 30 || value > 220`, so 220.5 is purged although ingest keeps it); the timestamp purge uses ingest's window; the repair touches `last > now + 1 day` (a cursor exactly one day ahead stays), every row including `hk:`, resetting to the newest sample at or before now or deleting, strips only `hk:` (a stuck `export:` row is deleted), and is not latched; the launch order prune → heart-rate purge → timestamp purge → repair; a step delta of zero or less writes nothing; a delta with no window start covers the day so far. | A store whose answers depend only on its arguments; Swift's integer guarantees kept. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/DailyStepsTest.kt`, `jvmTest/LocalStoreReadsTest.kt`, `jvmTest/RetentionAndRepairTest.kt`, `jvmTest/SyncCursorPlausibilityTest.kt`. |
| D-144 | **DEVIATION: the store's stored forms are its own JSON, read by one strict reader.** Upstream persists these types with Foundation's `JSONEncoder` / `JSONDecoder` (dates as seconds since 2001, keys in no fixed order). Here each type has a codec in `commonMain/codec/` (`SleepSegmentCodec`, `HistoryChannelTraceCodec`, `RingActivityEventLedgerCodec`, `HistoryFrameCaptureCodec`, `BatterySamplesCodec`, `RingAlarmCodec`, `WorkoutSessionSnapshotCodec`, `CursorSpanListCodec`, `SportSampleListCodec`, `LastFiredLedgerCodec`, `EnergyLedgerDayCodec`, `EpochArchiveCodec`, `StrandedCountersCodec`): dates are whole epoch milliseconds (D-137's rule), keys come in a fixed order, and `decode` returns `Decoded.Readable(value)` or `Decoded.Unreadable(raw, reason)` — it never throws and keeps the stored text. The last-fired ledgers, upstream property-list dictionaries of seconds or night keys, are JSON objects of whole epoch milliseconds or night keys, so a fractional value is the wrong type there. `RingAlarmCodec.encode`, `WorkoutSessionSnapshotCodec.encode` and `EnergyLedgerDayCodec.encode` return null for a value the stored form could not read back (a NaN or infinite double; for the energy day also a negative watermark) (upstream's alarm setter skips the save through `try?`; `encoded()` is nil); `WorkoutSessionSnapshotCodec.decodeOrNull(String?)` is upstream's `decoded(from: Data?)`. Stored text is parsed by the store itself (RFC 8259, nesting capped at 512), not by the serialization library's tree parser, which keeps the LAST of a duplicate key and accepts unquoted tokens such as `NaN` (measured on kotlinx-serialization-json 1.11.0). Kept as upstream, Foundation's decoder defaults measured on Swift 6.3.2: a missing or `null` required key is unreadable and a missing or `null` optional key is absent; an integer only from a number literal without a fraction (`1.0` and `1e3` read, `1.5`, `"7"` and `true` do not); past 64 bits unreadable; a double finite or unreadable (`1e400`); an enum only from a known raw string, with case; unknown keys ignored; a duplicate key keeps its FIRST value; one bad element fails the whole list; a battery percent outside 0–100, a weekday outside 1–7, an hour of -1 or a minute of 99 read as stored; -0 kcal keeps its sign. | The Android store never reads an iOS blob, so nothing needs Swift's date form or key order; one reader is one place for every rule, and an unreadable value returned as a value lets each caller decide what it costs (an empty ledger, no snapshot, an alarm kept as stored). | E6a (done): `jvmTest/codec/JsonReadTest.kt` (one test per rule, the library measurement, and 5 000 mutated inputs that never throw) and a clause test class per codec; the typed accessors over `store_kv` are D-149. |
| D-145 | **DEVIATION (bound): a stored integer past 32 bits makes the stored value unreadable.** Swift's `Int` is 64-bit, so upstream decodes an alarm hour or a workout reading count of 5 000 000 000 (measured). The Kotlin types hold these fields as `Int`: `RingAlarm.hour` / `minute` / `burstCount` and each weekday, `WorkoutSessionSnapshot.hrSampleCount` / `avgHR` / `maxHR`, `HistoryChannelTrace`'s counters, `BatteryTTE.Sample.percent`, `RingActivityEventLedger.Overflow.hidden` and `CapturedFrame.byteCount`. Such a value is never clamped or wrapped: the whole stored value is unreadable and its type's fail-closed rule applies (a snapshot becomes no snapshot, D-124). | Nothing this build writes is that large, so only a damaged value gets there; widening the ported types would change them for a value no Android build writes. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/codec/JsonReadTest.kt` and each codec's clause test class. |
| D-146 | **KEPT AS UPSTREAM, PINNED: a `null` sleep provenance reads as measured.** Upstream's hand-written decoder (`S/Metrics.swift:182-207`) checks `decodeNil` before it reads the label, so `"provenance": null` is "no label", that is `MEASURED` (measured on Swift 6.3.2); every other unreadable value — an unknown string, `3`, `{}`, `true`, `""` — is `ASSERTED_COVERAGE_UNKNOWN`, and the segment is kept. | A `null` reads like a missing key everywhere in the stored forms; the test makes a change deliberate. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/codec/SleepSegmentCodecTest.kt`. |
| D-147 | **DEVIATION (improvement): a stored frame capture is cross-checked and trimmed.** Upstream's synthesized decoder of `HistoryFrameCapture` keeps each frame's `hex`, `opcode` and `byteCount` as stored without comparing them, and it bypasses `init(frames:)`, so a stored capture of 1 600 frames decodes with all 1 600 (both measured). Here a frame's bytes are read from `hex`, which must be the lowercase, space-separated form the capture writes; a frame whose `opcode` or `byteCount` disagrees with its bytes makes the whole capture unreadable; and the capture is rebuilt through its constructor, which keeps the newest 1 500. | A diagnostic frame whose header and bytes disagree cannot be trusted for either; both cases need a capture this build did not write. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/codec/HistoryFrameCaptureCodecTest.kt`. |
| D-148 | **DEVIATION: the alarm's weekdays are written in ascending order.** Upstream encodes `weekdays` (a `Set`) in an order that changes from run to run (two different encodings in 20 runs, measured). Here they are written ascending, so one alarm always has one stored text. Reading is unchanged: a repeated day reads once, and a day outside 1–7 is kept (it never fires). | A stable stored text makes a saved alarm comparable byte for byte. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/codec/RingAlarmCodecTest.kt`. |
| D-149 | **DEVIATION: the values upstream keeps in `UserDefaults` live in one table of the store's database.** Upstream writes the activity-event ledger, the frame capture, the battery histories, the alert and notification ledgers, the alarm, the workout snapshot, the energy-ledger keys, the workout spans and sport samples, the epoch archive and the stranded counters to `UserDefaults`, each on its own. Here `BlobStore` reads and writes them in the `store_kv` table of the same Room database as the samples, one key each under upstream's key name (a per-ring value ends `/<ring id>`, where upstream appends `.<peripheral UUID>`; a ring id must be non-empty and contain no `/`). Each save joins the caller's transaction when one is open, so a value commits or rolls back with the samples and cursors written beside it; each save takes `now` for the row's update time instead of reading the clock. Loads fail closed as upstream's do: a ledger, capture, history, span list, sport-sample list, stranded ledger or last-fired ledger that cannot be read loads empty, and a workout snapshot loads as none; the alarm and the energy-ledger day report an unreadable value instead (D-150, D-151). A save whose value the stored form could not read back (NaN, infinity) returns false and leaves the stored value as it was, as upstream's `try?` / `encoded()` nil skip the write. Not given a home here: the history-sync evidence list and the pending sleep segments (their owners use the trace and segment codecs), `UnattributedPageBuffer` (in memory upstream), the `workout.inProgress` flag and the user settings (DataStore, E23). | One SQLite transaction can then hold the archive, the stranded counters and the ingest together (upstream's three separate writes can leave them out of step), and the clock stays a parameter (D-130's rule). | E6a (done): `jvmTest/BlobStoreTest.kt`, `jvmTest/EpochArchiveStoreTest.kt` (a transaction that throws leaves no blob and no sample). |
| D-150 | **DEVIATION (improvement): an unreadable stored alarm is kept and reported, never reset.** Upstream's getter returns `RingAlarm()` — 07:00, disabled — when it cannot decode the stored alarm (`A/RingAlarmController.swift:58-60`). Here `BlobStore.loadAlarm` returns `StoredAlarm.Unreadable(raw, reason)`, reading never writes, and the stored text stays byte for byte until `saveAlarm` stores a new alarm; a save that cannot be stored (a NaN burst spacing) leaves it too. `StoredAlarm.Absent` is "never saved". | One damaged value would otherwise silently turn the wearer's alarm off, found only by oversleeping. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/StoredAlarmKeptTest.kt`. |
| D-151 | **DEVIATION (improvement): the energy-ledger state is one stored value per day, and an unreadable day is reported.** Upstream keeps the ten `hk.activeEnergy.*` values as separate `UserDefaults` keys written one by one (`A/Health/HealthKitWriter.swift:1262-1286`, `:1596-1780`). Here they are one `EnergyLedgerDay` under `hk.activeEnergy/<local date>`, written whole. Decoding fails closed exactly where `ActiveEnergyLedger.plan` refuses to plan (D-79): a NaN, infinite or negative watermark, or a NaN or infinite carry, credit, saved or written total, is unreadable (a negative carry, credit or total is kept, as upstream); and `loadEnergyLedgerDay` returns `Decoded.Unreadable` — never null — for a stored day it cannot read, or for a readable day stored under another date's key. | The health store sums active energy: reading a damaged ledger as a fresh day would write the whole day again, permanently. The one-value day is the local unit a re-plan commits atomically; E10 owns the health-store-first write order around it. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/codec/EnergyLedgerDayCodecTest.kt`, `jvmTest/BlobStoreTest.kt`. |
| D-152 | **DEVIATION (bound): a stranded-epoch counter past 32 bits makes the ledger unreadable.** Upstream reads the stored counters with `NSNumber.uint32Value` (`A/Store/EpochArchiveStore.swift`), which wraps a larger value onto a different counter. Here a counter outside 0…0xFFFFFFFF, a fraction or a non-number makes the stored ledger unreadable, which loads empty, as upstream's own failed cast does; counters are written ascending. | A wrapped counter would select, and later retire, an epoch that was never stranded. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/codec/StrandedCountersCodecTest.kt`. |
| D-153 | **DEVIATION: the epoch archive and its drain facts are one stored value, and records reach it only through a bounded merge.** Upstream keeps the archive bytes, the last drain, the archive head, the unmoved-drain count and the HRV-pooling verdict as five keys (`A/Store/EpochArchiveStore.swift:37-44`). Here they are one `StoredEpochArchive` (the bytes as lowercase hex, read back with `EpochArchive.decode`); a drain fact that cannot be read reads as upstream's default for a missing key (no date, as for a stored time of 0 or less; 0 drains; no verdict) and never costs the records. `BlobStore.mergeEpochArchive` takes `notAfter: Instant` non-null and reports how many records were dated after it; no other store call writes records (`saveEpochArchiveMarks` replaces the facts only). An unreadable stored archive loads empty and the next merge replaces it, as upstream's decoder makes nothing of bytes it cannot read. | The archive cannot be fetched again, so the bound of D-44 is enforced where it is stored, not left to each caller; one value keeps the records and their facts in step. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/codec/EpochArchiveCodecTest.kt`, `jvmTest/EpochArchiveStoreTest.kt`. E9 passes the current time plus a clock-skew allowance. |
| D-154 | **DEVIATION (improvement): a move whose original entry has vanished keeps the entry already at the destination.** Upstream's `savePeriodEntry` and `saveHeadacheEntry` (`A/CycleStore.swift:89-112`, `A/Store/HeadacheStore.swift:218-245`) delete the row at the new start / onset first, then look for the original; when the original is gone (an edit screen opened on an entry that has since been deleted or moved) they fall through to a plain save, and the destination row — with the ids of the Health samples written for it — is lost. Here the destination row is folded away only when the original is there to take its ids; otherwise the plain save edits it in place under the usual clinical-change rule. With the original present, the outcome is upstream's. | A deleted row's Health sample ids can never be named again, so the samples stay in the health store for good; a stale edit screen is an ordinary event. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/UserEntryStoreTest.kt` (`aMoveFromAVanishedOriginalKeepsTheRowAlreadyAtTheDestination`, red with upstream's order). |
| D-155 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the period, headache and risk-day paths, and outcomes kept as upstream.** Shape: each call is one transaction on `LocalStore` and takes the time it stamps (`now`; upstream reads the clock), the rows stay internal and callers get `PeriodEntry` / `HeadacheEntry` (built by the store only, their lists its own) / `HeadacheRiskDay` values; `insertRiskDayIfAbsent` takes a `HeadacheRiskDay` whose `computedAt` / `updatedAt` the caller states; `markRiskRestaged` / `markRiskAlerted` take `now`. Every instant is cut to the stored millisecond before it is compared or looked up, so a sub-millisecond difference is neither a clinical change nor a move. A failed fetch fails the write (upstream's `try?` reads it as "no row"). Columns whose upstream default reads the clock (`updatedAt = Date()`, `computedAt = Date()`) default to `distantPast`. `[String]` columns are JSON arrays in text: a list that would not read back as itself (a lone surrogate, which no Swift string can hold) is refused on write, and a damaged list fails the read instead of reading as empty. The move and clinical-change rules, which upstream writes out twice, are one implementation over both tables. Kept as upstream: a move appends the displaced row's ids to the original's (`+=`, not a set) and clears the watermark; clinical = flow / end / symptoms for a period, severity / end / symptoms for a headache (notes, custom symptoms and triggers are not), compared with order; an edit keeps the entry's source and imported id; ranges are `[from, to)`; the risk row is refused for a scored day or a scored night other than `distantPast`, `riskRow(distantPast)` is null, a re-stage is recorded once and never touches the score, marks on a day with no row do nothing; `flowLabel` / `severityLabel` are not store concerns (the UI's). | The store reads no clock; the millisecond cut is the store's one time rule (D-137); one copy of the move rule cannot drift from the other; a silent empty Health-id list would strand samples. | E6a (done): `jvmTest/HeadacheStoreTest.kt`, `jvmTest/UserEntryStoreTest.kt`, `jvmTest/ListColumnConverterTest.kt`, `jvmTest/StoredUserTablesColumnsTest.kt`. |
